\boxed{|R_{n}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m)\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}\,\frac{\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}}{n}}

(D.27)

with probability
\geq 1-\bar{d}^{-c}
. This is the bound stated in Theorem
5.1
. Crucially,
no
C_{A}
appears
: the score-whitening construction eliminates the operator inverse entirely.

Proof of Theorem
D.4
.

Sum the bounds (i)–(v):

•

Term (i) contributes
\sqrt{\bar{d}}\,\rho\sqrt{\log/n}
(variance) and
\bar{d}\log/n
(sub-exponential tail).

•

Term (ii) contributes
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}
(first-order piece) and
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}
(second-order, lower order).

•

Term (iii) contributes
\rho^{2}
.

•

Term (iv) contributes
\sqrt{\bar{d}}\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\rho
(first order) and
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
(second order).

•

Term (v): first order cancels completely; second-order remainder contributes
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
.

Combining yields (
D.26
).

\square

E
Proofs for IPW and Nonlinear Extensions

This section provides the detailed proofs for the IPW and nonlinear extensions in Section
5
: IPW with known weights (§
E.1
), IPW with estimated weights (§
E.2
), and nonlinear functionals (§
E.3
).
Throughout, we work under the comparison design and Assumption
C.3
.

E.1
IPW with known weights

We verify that the five-term decomposition of Appendix
D
carries through when the whitened score
\tilde{s}
is replaced by the IPW-whitened score
\tilde{s}^{w}(y,\eta;x):=w(x)\tilde{s}(y,\eta)
, where
w(x)=q(x)/p(x)
and
q
is the uniform reference distribution.

E.1.1
Setup

The key structural fact, established in the main text (
5.12
), is that the effective Gram operator under the IPW score satisfies

G_{q}(H)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim p}\!\big[w(X)\left\langle H,X\right\rangle X\big]=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim q}\!\big[\left\langle H,X\right\rangle X\big]=\frac{1}{d^{\star}}H

on the column-sum-zero subspace.
This is identical to
G_{0}=(1/d^{\star})I
from Appendix
D
.
The oracle and estimated directions are therefore the same as (
5.13
):

H_{q,0}=d^{\star}\,P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma,\qquad\hat{H}_{q,0}=d^{\star}\,\widehat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma,

which coincide with
H^{\star}_{\rm ws}
and
\hat{H}_{\rm ws}
.

E.1.2
Remainder decomposition

The total remainder
R_{n}^{\rm ipw}
for the IPW estimator (
5.14
) decomposes exactly as (
D.25
):

R_{n}^{\rm ipw}=R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\tilde{H},w}+R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\tilde{\eta},w}+R_{\mathrm{proj}}+\text{H-bias}^{w}+\tilde{R}_{2}^{w}(\Delta;H^{\star}_{\rm ws}),

(E.1)

where each “
w
”-superscripted term is the same as the corresponding term in Appendix
D
with
\tilde{s}
replaced by
w(X)\tilde{s}
.

E.1.3
Effect of the weight factor

Each random variable in the decomposition acquires at most an additional multiplicative factor of
|w(X)|
.
Under the overlap condition (
5.15
),
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq C_{p}/c_{p}=:C_{w}
, which is a dimension-free constant.
Specifically:

(i)

Direction-error empirical process
R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\tilde{H},w}
:
The summand
Z_{i}^{w}=w(X_{i})\tilde{s}(Y_{i},\eta^{\star}_{i})\left\langle\hat{H}_{\rm ws}-H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X_{i}\right\rangle
satisfies

\|Z_{i}^{w}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq C_{w}\,\|Z_{i}\|_{\psi_{1}},\qquad\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i}^{w})\leq C_{w}^{2}\,\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i}).

Therefore Theorem
D.1
applies with
C(\mu,r,m)
replaced by
C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})
.

(ii)

Score-perturbation
R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\tilde{\eta},w}
:
The Taylor expansion of
w(X)\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})
around
\eta^{\star}
produces the same structure as Section
D.3
with each derivative
\partial_{\eta}^{k}\tilde{s}
multiplied by
w(X)
.
Since
|w(X)\partial_{\eta}^{k}\tilde{s}|\leq C_{w}|\partial_{\eta}^{k}\tilde{s}|
, Corollary
D.2
holds with a constant-factor change.

(iii)

Projection leakage
R_{\mathrm{proj}}
:
This term is independent of the score (Eq. (
D.17
)); it remains unchanged.

(iv)

H-direction bias
:
The population expectation is now
\mathbb{E}_{p}[w(X)\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\left\langle\hat{H}_{\rm ws}-H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle]=\mathbb{E}_{q}[\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\left\langle\hat{H}_{\rm ws}-H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle]
,
which is the uniform-design expectation.
All bounds from Section
D.5
apply verbatim (the change of measure from
p
to
q
via
w
is exact, not approximate).

(v)

First-order cancellation and second-order remainder
:
The first-order cancellation uses
\mathbb{E}_{p}[w(X)\partial_{\eta}\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})\mid X]=-w(X)
and the Gram identity
G_{q}=G_{0}=(1/d^{\star})I
, giving the same complete cancellation (
D.22
).
The second-order remainder acquires at most a factor
C_{w}
:

|\tilde{R}_{2}^{w}|\leq C_{w}\,C(\mu,r,m)\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}
.

Corollary E.1
(Combined IPW remainder bound).

Under Assumption
C.3
, the overlap condition (
5.15
) with known weights, and the subspace estimation guarantees (P1)–(P5), with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle|R_{n}^{\rm ipw}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}\bigg[

\displaystyle\sqrt{\bar{d}}\;\rho\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\;+\;\bar{d}\,\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n}\;+\;

(E.2)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}}\;+\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\rho\;+\;\rho^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\bigg].

This is identical to Theorem
D.4
with
C(\mu,r,m)
replaced by
C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})
, where
C_{w}=C_{p}/c_{p}
depends only on the overlap constants.

Proof.

Sum the five individual bounds (i)–(v) above.
Each acquires at most
C_{w}^{2}
(for variance) or
C_{w}
(for tail/bias) compared to the corresponding bound in Theorem
D.4
.
All structural cancellations (isotropic Gram, complete first-order cancellation) are preserved.

\square

E.2
IPW with estimated weights

Suppose the estimated sampling distribution
\hat{p}
satisfies the entrywise relative error guarantee

\delta_{w}\;:=\;\|\hat{w}-w\|_{\infty}\;\leq\;C_{w}\,\epsilon_{p},\qquad\epsilon_{p}\;\ll\;1,

(E.3)

where
\hat{w}(x):=q(x)/\hat{p}(x)
and
C_{w}:=C_{p}/c_{p}
is the overlap ratio.

E.2.1
Decomposition

The feasible IPW remainder decomposes as

R_{n}^{\rm ipw,\hat{w}}\;=\;\underbrace{R_{n}^{\rm ipw}}_{\text{known-weight}}\;+\;\underbrace{R_{n}^{\Delta w}}_{\text{weight-estimation error}},

(E.4)

where
R_{n}^{\rm ipw}
is bounded in Corollary
E.1
. The weight-estimation remainder further splits as

R_{n}^{\Delta w}\;=\;\underbrace{B^{\Delta w}}_{\text{population bias}}\;+\;\underbrace{R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\Delta w}}_{\text{empirical process}}.

(E.5)

E.2.2
Population bias

The leading-order weight error vanishes by score centering:

\mathbb{P}^{\star}\big[(\hat{w}(X)-w(X))\,\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})\,\left\langle H^{\star}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big]\;=\;0.

(E.6)

What remains is driven by the score perturbation
\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})-\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})
and the direction error
\hat{H}_{q,0}-H^{\star}_{q,0}
, both of which are second-order.

First component.

By Taylor expansion and the complete first-order cancellation (
D.22
):

\big|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\big[\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\,\left\langle H^{\star}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big]\big|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m)\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}.

Second component.

By the
\ell_{1}
-average bound (Lemma
C.14
) and the direction-error estimate:

\big|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\big[\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\,\left\langle\hat{H}_{q,0}-H^{\star}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big]\big|\;\leq\;C_{\tilde{s}}\,\frac{\left\lVert\hat{H}_{q,0}-H^{\star}_{q,0}\right\rVert_{1}}{d^{\star}}\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m)\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}\,(\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\rho+\rho^{2}).

Combined bias bound.

|B^{\Delta w}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\delta_{w}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}\,\big[\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\rho+\rho^{2}\big].

(E.7)

E.2.3
Empirical process

The empirical process term is

R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\Delta w}\;=\;(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P}^{\star})\big[(\hat{w}(X)-w(X))\,\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\,\left\langle\hat{H}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big].

Factoring out
\delta_{w}
and using the Frobenius reduction:

Variance bound.

\mathrm{Var}\big((\hat{w}-w)\tilde{s}\,\left\langle\hat{H}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big)\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\delta_{w}^{2}\,\bar{d}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}^{2}.

(E.8)

Sub-exponential norm.

\big\|(\hat{w}-w)\tilde{s}\,\left\langle\hat{H}_{q,0},X\right\rangle\big\|_{\psi_{1}}\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\delta_{w}\,\bar{d}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}.

(E.9)

Bernstein bound.

By Lemma
C.39
, with probability at least
1-\delta
:

|R_{\mathrm{emp}}^{\Delta w}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\delta_{w}\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\;+\;\bar{d}\,\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n}\right].

(E.10)

Since
\delta_{w}\leq C_{w}\epsilon_{p}=O(\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{par}}/n})
with
d_{\mathrm{par}}\ll d^{\star}
, this is lower order than the known-weight terms.

E.2.4
Combined bound

Theorem E.2
(Feasible IPW remainder bound).

Under Assumption
C.3
, the overlap condition, and the weight estimation guarantee (
E.3
) with
\epsilon_{p}\leq 1/2
, with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle|R_{n}^{\rm ipw,\hat{w}}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,C_{w})\,\left\lVert\Gamma\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\bigg[\sqrt{\bar{d}}\;\rho\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\;+\;\bar{d}\,\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n}\;+\;

(E.11)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}}\;+\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\rho\;+\;\rho^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+\epsilon_{p}\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\bigg].

The weight-estimation term
\epsilon_{p}\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}
is comparable to
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
when
d_{\mathrm{par}}\ll d^{\star}
.

Proof.

By (
E.4
),
R_{n}^{\rm ipw,\hat{w}}=R_{n}^{\rm ipw}+R_{n}^{\Delta w}
.
The first term is bounded by Corollary
E.1
.
For the second: the population bias (
E.7
) contributes
O(\epsilon_{p})
times the existing second-order terms; the empirical process (
E.10
) contributes
\delta_{w}
times the leading empirical terms.
Both carry an extra factor
\epsilon_{p}=O(\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{par}}/n})
and are lower order.

\square

E.3
Nonlinear functionals

We prove Theorem
5.5
by reducing the nonlinear problem to the linear score-whitened theorem (Theorem
D.4
) plus two explicit second-order corrections.

E.3.1
Setup and exact decomposition

Let
\Gamma_{\psi}:=\nabla\psi(T^{\star})
,
\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}:=\nabla\psi(\hat{T})
, and
\Delta:=\hat{T}-T^{\star}
.
Decompose the plug-in direction as

\hat{H}_{\psi}=\underbrace{d^{\star}\widehat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma_{\psi}}_{=:\hat{H}_{\psi}^{(a)}}+\underbrace{d^{\star}\widehat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi})}_{=:\hat{H}_{\psi}^{(b)}},

(E.12)

and define the
linearized estimator with the true gradient
:

\hat{\psi}_{\mathrm{lin},\psi}:=\left\langle\Gamma_{\psi},\hat{T}\right\rangle+\mathbb{P}_{n}\big[\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\left\langle\hat{H}_{\psi}^{(a)},X\right\rangle\big].

Adding and subtracting
\hat{\psi}_{\mathrm{lin},\psi}
gives the exact identity

\boxed{\hat{\psi}_{\rm nl}-\psi(T^{\star})\;=\;\underbrace{\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{\mathrm{lin},\psi}-\left\langle\Gamma_{\psi},T^{\star}\right\rangle\bigr)}_{\text{linearized estimator}}\;+\;\underbrace{R_{\mathrm{plug},\psi}}_{\text{plug-in Taylor remainder}}\;+\;\underbrace{R_{\nabla,\psi}}_{\text{gradient perturbation}},}

(E.13)

where

R_{\mathrm{plug},\psi}:=\psi(\hat{T})-\psi(T^{\star})-\left\langle\Gamma_{\psi},\Delta\right\rangle,\qquad R_{\nabla,\psi}:=\mathbb{P}_{n}\big[\tilde{s}(Y,\hat{\eta})\left\langle\hat{H}_{\psi}^{(b)},X\right\rangle\big].

E.3.2
Term 1: Linearized estimator (leading term)

The first term in (
E.13
) is exactly the linear score-whitened estimator with the deterministic gradient
\Gamma_{\psi}
replacing
\Gamma
. By Theorem
D.4
:

\hat{\psi}_{\mathrm{lin},\psi}-\left\langle\Gamma_{\psi},T^{\star}\right\rangle\;=\;(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P}^{\star})\tilde{\phi}_{\psi}^{\star}+R_{n}^{\mathrm{lin}}(\Gamma_{\psi}),

(E.14)

where
\tilde{\phi}_{\psi}^{\star}(X,Y):=\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})\left\langle d^{\star}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma_{\psi},X\right\rangle
and

|R_{n}^{\mathrm{lin}}(\Gamma_{\psi})|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m)\,\left\lVert\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{1}\,B_{n},

(E.15)

with

B_{n}:=\sqrt{\bar{d}\,\rho\,\log(2/\delta)/n}+\bar{d}\,\log(2/\delta)/n+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,\rho+\rho^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
.
Since
\Gamma_{\psi}
is supported on
S_{\psi}
with
|S_{\psi}|=s_{\psi}=O(1)
, we have
\left\lVert\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\sqrt{s_{\psi}}\,\|\Gamma_{\psi}\|_{F}=O(1)
, so the linearized remainder is of the same order as in the linear problem.

E.3.3
Term 2: Plug-in Taylor remainder

Under Assumption
5.4
,
\psi(T)=g(T_{S_{\psi}})
for a twice differentiable
g:\mathbb{R}^{s_{\psi}}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Write
z^{\star}:=T^{\star}_{S_{\psi}}
and
\hat{z}:=\hat{T}_{S_{\psi}}=z^{\star}+\Delta_{S_{\psi}}
.
By the second-order Taylor formula in
\mathbb{R}^{s_{\psi}}
, there exists
\tilde{z}
on the segment joining
z^{\star}
and
\hat{z}
such that

R_{\mathrm{plug},\psi}\;=\;g(\hat{z})-g(z^{\star})-\nabla g(z^{\star})^{\top}(\hat{z}-z^{\star})\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Delta_{S_{\psi}}^{\top}\,\nabla^{2}g(\tilde{z})\,\Delta_{S_{\psi}}.

Using
\|\nabla^{2}g(\tilde{z})\|_{\rm op}\leq L_{g}
and
\|\Delta_{S_{\psi}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq s_{\psi}\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
:

\boxed{|R_{\mathrm{plug},\psi}|\;\leq\;\tfrac{1}{2}L_{g}\,s_{\psi}\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\;\lesssim\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}.}

(E.16)

This is genuinely second-order because
\psi
depends on only
s_{\psi}=O(1)
entries: the finite-support condition ensures that the Taylor remainder involves only a bounded-dimensional Hessian applied to a vector of length
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}
.

E.3.4
Term 3: Gradient perturbation

The gradient perturbation direction is
\hat{H}_{\psi}^{(b)}=d^{\star}\widehat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi})
.
Since
\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi}
is supported on
S_{\psi}
and the mean-value theorem gives
\left\lVert\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq L_{g}\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}
:

\left\lVert\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{1}\;\leq\;s_{\psi}\,L_{g}\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\;\lesssim\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(E.17)

The term
R_{\nabla,\psi}
has the same structure as the linear score-whitened one-step correction with
\hat{\Gamma}_{\psi}-\Gamma_{\psi}
replacing
\Gamma
.
Applying the linear theorem:

|R_{\nabla,\psi}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,L_{g},s_{\psi})\,\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,B_{n}.

(E.18)

This is
O(\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty})
times the linearized remainder (
E.15
) and hence strictly lower order.

E.3.5
Combined bound

Theorem E.3
(Remainder bound for nonlinear one-step estimator).

Under Assumption
C.3
, Assumption
5.4
, and the subspace estimation guarantees (P1)–(P5), with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\boxed{|R_{n}^{\rm nl}|\;\leq\;C(\mu,r,m,L_{g},s_{\psi})\Big[\left\lVert\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{1}\,B_{n}\;+\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\,B_{n}\;+\;\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\Big],}

(E.19)

where
B_{n}
is as in (
E.15
).

Proof.

Sum the three terms from (
E.13
): the linearized remainder (
E.15
), the plug-in Taylor remainder (
E.16
), and the gradient perturbation (
E.18
).
Since
\left\lVert\Gamma_{\psi}\right\rVert_{1}=O(1)
, the linearized term dominates.
The gradient-perturbation term carries an extra factor
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}=o(1)
and is lower order.
The plug-in Taylor remainder is
O(\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2})=O(\bar{d}/n)
, which is also lower order.

\square

CLT.

Under the pairwise specialization
\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}/n}
, all three remainder terms are
O(\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}/n)
, giving
\sqrt{n}R_{n}^{\rm nl}\to 0
under
n\gg\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}
. Hence

\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\psi}_{\rm nl}-\psi(T^{\star})\big)\;\xrightarrow{d}\;\mathcal{N}(0,\;V_{\rm ws}(\psi)),\qquad V_{\rm ws}(\psi)=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\left[\frac{\left\langle d^{\star}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma_{\psi},X\right\rangle^{2}}{I(\eta^{\star})}\right].

F
Berry–Esseen Bound for the Pairwise-Comparison Estimators

This section establishes a quantitative Berry–Esseen bound for the one-step estimators.
The CLT convergence itself follows from a standard conditional Lyapunov CLT and Slutsky’s theorem once the remainder conditions of Theorems
4.4
and
5.2
are verified (as sketched in the main text).
Here we go further and derive an explicit rate of convergence for the Kolmogorov distance.
The argument proceeds in two stages: first, we bound the Berry–Esseen error of the leading i.i.d. term (Subsection
F.1
); then we quantify the additional error contributed by the one-step remainder
R_{n}
(Subsection
F.4
).

We use the following regularity conditions throughout, all of which hold under the bounded-signal condition
\|T^{\star}\|_{\infty}\leq B
of the main text:

(R1)

Fisher information bounds:
there exist
0<c_{I}\leq C_{I}<\infty
such that
c_{I}\leq I(\eta^{\star})\leq C_{I}
uniformly over all admissible
X
;

(R2)

Third-moment bounds:
there exist
M_{3,\rm ws},M_{3,\rm eff}<\infty
such that
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[|\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})|^{3}\mid X]\leq M_{3,\rm ws}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[|s(Y,\eta^{\star})|^{3}\mid X]\leq M_{3,\rm eff}
for all admissible
X
;

(R3)

Tangent-projection bound:

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|P_{\mathbb{T}}x\|_{F}\leq C_{\rm proj}\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}
.

F.1
Berry–Esseen bound for the leading i.i.d. term

Proposition F.1
(Berry–Esseen for the whitened leading term).

Let
Z_{i}:=\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{\star}(X_{i},Y_{i})=\tilde{s}(Y_{i},\eta_{i}^{\star})\,\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X_{i}\right\rangle
with
H^{\star}_{\rm ws}=d^{\star}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
, and let
v_{\rm ws}^{2}:=\mathrm{Var}(Z_{1})
. Under conditions
(R1)–(R3)
and the isotropic design assumption, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
c_{I},C_{I},M_{3,\rm ws}
, and
C_{\rm proj}
, such that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\,v_{\rm ws}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;C\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}}.

We divide the argument into four steps.

Step 1: mean zero.

Because the whitened score
\tilde{s}(y,\eta)=s(y,\eta)/I(\eta)
is conditionally centered,

\mathbb{E}^{\star}[\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})\mid X]=0,

we have

\mathbb{E}^{\star}Z_{1}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\Big(\mathbb{E}^{\star}[\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})\mid X]\,\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle\Big)=0.

Hence
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}
are i.i.d. mean-zero random variables.

Step 2: second moment.

By definition,
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[s(Y,\eta^{\star})^{2}\mid X]=I(\eta^{\star})
, so

\mathbb{E}^{\star}[\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})^{2}\mid X]=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\star}[s(Y,\eta^{\star})^{2}\mid X]}{I(\eta^{\star})^{2}}=\frac{1}{I(\eta^{\star})}.

Therefore, by the tower property,

v_{\rm ws}^{2}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}[Z_{1}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\left[\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})^{2}\,\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\left[\frac{\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle^{2}}{I(\eta^{\star})}\right].

Using condition (R1) for the sandwich
1/C_{I}\leq 1/I(\eta^{\star})\leq 1/c_{I}
, together with the isotropic design identity
\mathbb{E}^{\star}\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle^{2}=\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{2}/d^{\star}
(valid because
H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\in\mathbb{T}
):

\frac{1}{C_{I}}\frac{\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{2}}{d^{\star}}\;\leq\;v_{\rm ws}^{2}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{c_{I}}\frac{\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{2}}{d^{\star}}.

(F.1)

In particular, the cube of the standard deviation satisfies

v_{\rm ws}^{3}\;\geq\;C_{I}^{-3/2}\,\frac{\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{3}}{(d^{\star})^{3/2}}.

(F.2)

Step 3: third absolute moment.

Starting from
\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}|^{3}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\big[|\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})|^{3}\,|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle|^{3}\big]
, we condition on
X
and apply (R2):

\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}|^{3}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\Big(\mathbb{E}^{\star}[|\tilde{s}(Y,\eta^{\star})|^{3}\mid X]\,|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle|^{3}\Big)\;\leq\;M_{3,\rm ws}\,\mathbb{E}^{\star}|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle|^{3}.

(F.3)

We now bound
\mathbb{E}^{\star}|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle|^{3}
using the elementary inequality

\mathbb{E}|W|^{3}\;\leq\;\big(\sup|W|\big)\,\mathbb{E}|W|^{2},

(F.4)

applied with
W=\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle
.
Because
H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\in\mathbb{T}
, the inner product satisfies
\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},x\right\rangle=\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},P_{\mathbb{T}}x\right\rangle
, so by Cauchy–Schwarz and condition (R3):

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},x\right\rangle|\;\leq\;\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}\,\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|P_{\mathbb{T}}x\|_{F}\;\leq\;C_{\rm proj}\,\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}.

(F.5)

The isotropic design identity gives
\mathbb{E}^{\star}\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle^{2}=\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{2}/d^{\star}
. Substituting (
F.5
) and this identity into (
F.4
):

\mathbb{E}^{\star}|\left\langle H^{\star}_{\rm ws},X\right\rangle|^{3}\;\leq\;C_{\rm proj}\,\frac{\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{3}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}}}{(d^{\star})^{3/2}}.

(F.6)

Combining (
F.3
) and (
F.6
):

\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}|^{3}\;\leq\;M_{3,\rm ws}\,C_{\rm proj}\,\frac{\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}^{3}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}}}{(d^{\star})^{3/2}}.

(F.7)

Step 4: standardized third moment and Berry–Esseen.

Dividing (
F.7
) by the lower bound (
F.2
):

\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}|^{3}}{v_{\rm ws}^{3}}\;\leq\;M_{3,\rm ws}\,C_{\rm proj}\,C_{I}^{3/2}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}}\;=:\;C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}}.

(F.8)

This is the key bound: the standardized third absolute moment is only
O(\sqrt{\bar{d}})
.

Applying the classical Berry–Esseen theorem to the i.i.d. mean-zero variables
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}
:

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\,v_{\rm ws}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;\frac{C_{\rm BE}}{\sqrt{n}}\,\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}|^{3}}{v_{\rm ws}^{3}}\;\leq\;\frac{C_{\rm BE}\,C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}}}{\sqrt{n}}=C\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}},

(F.9)

where
C=C_{\rm BE}\,C_{0}=C_{\rm BE}\,M_{3,\rm ws}\,C_{\rm proj}\,C_{I}^{3/2}
and
C_{\rm BE}\leq 0.4748
is the universal Berry–Esseen constant.
∎

F.2
Tangent-projection bound for pairwise atoms

We verify that condition (R3) follows from the standard basis-tensor projection bound.

Lemma F.2
(Pairwise tangent-projection bound).

Suppose that for every canonical basis tensor
E_{\omega}
,

\|P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega}\|_{F}\;\leq\;C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}.

Then for every pairwise-comparison design tensor
X=E_{\omega^{+}}-E_{\omega^{-}}
,

\|P_{\mathbb{T}}X\|_{F}\;\leq\;2C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}.

Hence condition
(R3)
holds with
C_{\rm proj}=2C_{0}
.

By linearity of
P_{\mathbb{T}}
and the triangle inequality,

\|P_{\mathbb{T}}X\|_{F}=\|P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega^{+}}-P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega^{-}}\|_{F}\;\leq\;\|P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega^{+}}\|_{F}+\|P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega^{-}}\|_{F}\;\leq\;2C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}.

The basis-tensor bound
\|P_{\mathbb{T}}E_{\omega}\|_{F}\leq C_{0}\sqrt{\bar{d}/d^{\star}}
is the standard
\mu
-incoherence bound from the tensor-completion literature, and holds with
C_{0}=C(\mu,r,m)
. The pairwise design introduces only an absolute constant factor
2
.
∎

F.3
Berry–Esseen bound for the efficient leading term

The same argument applies, with minimal modification, to the efficient (non-whitened) leading term.

Proposition F.3
(Berry–Esseen for the efficient leading term).

Let
Z_{i}^{\rm eff}:=\phi^{\star}(X_{i},Y_{i})=s(Y_{i},\eta_{i}^{\star})\,\left\langle H^{\star},X_{i}\right\rangle
with
H^{\star}=A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
, and let
v_{\rm eff}^{2}:=\mathrm{Var}(Z_{1}^{\rm eff})
. Under conditions
(R1)–(R3)
and the isotropic design assumption,

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\,v_{\rm eff}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{\rm eff}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;C\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}}.

The proof follows the same four-step structure as Proposition
F.1
; we record only the changes.

Step 1.

Mean zero follows from
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[s(Y,\eta^{\star})\mid X]=0
.

Step 2.

Since
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[s(Y,\eta^{\star})^{2}\mid X]=I(\eta^{\star})
, the second moment is

v_{\rm eff}^{2}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\big[I(\eta^{\star})\,\left\langle H^{\star},X\right\rangle^{2}\big].

Using condition (R1) and the isotropic design:

\frac{c_{I}}{d^{\star}}\|H^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}\;\leq\;v_{\rm eff}^{2}\;\leq\;\frac{C_{I}}{d^{\star}}\|H^{\star}\|_{F}^{2}.

(F.10)

Step 3.

Using condition (R2),
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[|s|^{3}\mid X]\leq M_{3,\rm eff}
, and the same decomposition
\mathbb{E}|W|^{3}\leq(\sup|W|)\mathbb{E}|W|^{2}
:

\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}^{\rm eff}|^{3}\;\leq\;M_{3,\rm eff}\,C_{\rm proj}\,\frac{\|H^{\star}\|_{F}^{3}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}}}{(d^{\star})^{3/2}}.

(F.11)

Step 4.

Dividing by
v_{\rm eff}^{3}\geq c_{I}^{3/2}\|H^{\star}\|_{F}^{3}/(d^{\star})^{3/2}
(from (
F.10
)):

\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\star}|Z_{1}^{\rm eff}|^{3}}{v_{\rm eff}^{3}}\;\leq\;\frac{M_{3,\rm eff}\,C_{\rm proj}}{c_{I}^{3/2}}\,\sqrt{\bar{d}},

and the Berry–Esseen theorem gives the stated bound. The
\|H^{\star}\|_{F}
terms cancel exactly as before, so the leading-term Berry–Esseen rate is the same
O(\sqrt{\bar{d}/n})
for both the whitened and efficient estimators.
∎

F.4
Combined Berry–Esseen bound with remainder

The one-step decomposition
\hat{\psi}_{n}-\psi(T^{\star})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}+R_{n}
contains both the i.i.d. leading term and the remainder
R_{n}
.
The leading-term Berry–Esseen bounds of Propositions
F.1
and
F.3
quantify the Gaussian approximation error of the first part.
We now derive the full Berry–Esseen bound by carefully incorporating the remainder, which is random and dependent on the same evaluation-fold data.

Theorem F.4
(Combined Berry–Esseen bound).

Let
T_{n}:=\sqrt{n}(\hat{\psi}_{n}-\psi(T^{\star}))/v
denote the standardized one-step estimator, where
v=v_{\rm ws}
or
v_{\rm eff}
as appropriate.
Write
T_{n}=S_{n}+\rho_{n}
where

S_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\,v}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{n}:=\frac{\sqrt{n}\,R_{n}}{v}

are the standardized leading term and standardized remainder, respectively.
Let
r_{n}>0
be any deterministic bound and define the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}:=\{|\rho_{n}|\leq r_{n}\}
.
Then

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\left|P\!\left(T_{n}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;\Delta_{\rm BE}+\frac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}),

(F.12)

where
\Delta_{\rm BE}=C\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}
is the leading-term Berry–Esseen error from Proposition
F.1

(
resp.
F.3
)
.

Fix any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since
T_{n}=S_{n}+\rho_{n}
, we decompose the probability according to the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
.

Upper bound.

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
,
|\rho_{n}|\leq r_{n}
, so
T_{n}\leq t
implies
S_{n}=T_{n}-\rho_{n}\leq t+r_{n}
. Therefore

P(T_{n}\leq t,\;\mathcal{E}_{n})\;\leq\;P(S_{n}\leq t+r_{n}).

Adding the failure probability:

\displaystyle P(T_{n}\leq t)

\displaystyle\;=\;P(T_{n}\leq t,\;\mathcal{E}_{n})+P(T_{n}\leq t,\;\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\;\leq\;P(S_{n}\leq t+r_{n})+P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}).

(F.13)

Now apply the Berry–Esseen bound from Proposition
F.1
(or
F.3
), which holds uniformly in
u
:

P(S_{n}\leq t+r_{n})\;\leq\;\Phi(t+r_{n})+\Delta_{\rm BE}.

Since
\Phi
is Lipschitz with constant
\sup_{t}\Phi^{\prime}(t)=\sup_{t}\phi(t)=1/\sqrt{2\pi}
,

\Phi(t+r_{n})\;\leq\;\Phi(t)+\frac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}.

Substituting into (
F.13
):

P(T_{n}\leq t)\;\leq\;\Phi(t)+\Delta_{\rm BE}+\frac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}).

(F.14)

Lower bound.

On
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, if
S_{n}\leq t-r_{n}
then
T_{n}=S_{n}+\rho_{n}\leq(t-r_{n})+r_{n}=t
. Therefore

P(S_{n}\leq t-r_{n},\;\mathcal{E}_{n})\;\leq\;P(T_{n}\leq t).

Since
P(S_{n}\leq t-r_{n},\;\mathcal{E}_{n})\geq P(S_{n}\leq t-r_{n})-P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c})
:

\displaystyle P(T_{n}\leq t)

\displaystyle\;\geq\;P(S_{n}\leq t-r_{n})-P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\;\geq\;\Phi(t-r_{n})-\Delta_{\rm BE}-P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\;\geq\;\Phi(t)-\frac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-\Delta_{\rm BE}-P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}).

(F.15)

Conclusion.

Combining (
F.14
) and (
F.15
):

\left|P(T_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;\Delta_{\rm BE}+\frac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}).

Taking the supremum over
t\in\mathbb{R}
gives (
F.12
).
∎

We now specialize to each estimator.

Whitened estimator.

By Theorem
5.2
, with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-c}
,

|R_{n}|\;\leq\;C\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}\,\frac{\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}}{n}.

The second-moment bound (
F.1
) gives
v_{\rm ws}\geq C_{I}^{-1/2}\|H^{\star}_{\rm ws}\|_{F}/\sqrt{d^{\star}}\asymp\sqrt{\bar{d}}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}
.
Therefore the standardized remainder satisfies, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
,

|\rho_{n}|=\frac{\sqrt{n}\,|R_{n}|}{v_{\rm ws}}\;\leq\;C^{\prime}\,\frac{\sqrt{n}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}\,\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}/n}{\sqrt{\bar{d}}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}}=C^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}}{n}}\;=:\;r_{n},

with
P(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c})\leq\bar{d}^{-c}
.
Substituting into (
F.12
) with
\Delta_{\rm BE}=C\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}
from Proposition
F.1
:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\left|P\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\psi}_{n}^{\rm ws}-\psi(T^{\star}))}{v_{\rm ws}}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|

(F.16)

\displaystyle\leq\;\underbrace{C\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}}{n}}}_{\text{leading-term BE}}\;+\;\underbrace{\frac{C^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}}{n}}}_{\text{remainder contribution}}\;+\;\underbrace{\bar{d}^{-c}}_{\text{failure prob.}}\;=\;O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}}{n}}\right).

This vanishes under the sample-size condition
n\gg\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}
, which is the same condition required for the CLT.
Since
c>0
can be taken arbitrarily large, the failure probability
\bar{d}^{-c}
is negligible compared to the other two terms.

Efficient estimator.

By Theorem
4.4
, with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-c}
,

|R_{n}|\;\leq\;C\,C_{A}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}\,\frac{\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}}{n}.

Since
v_{\rm eff}\asymp\sqrt{\bar{d}}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}
by (
F.10
), the standardized remainder satisfies

|\rho_{n}|\;\leq\;C_{A}\cdot C^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}}{n}}\;=:\;r_{n}.

Substituting with
\Delta_{\rm BE}=C\sqrt{\bar{d}/n}
from Proposition
F.3
:

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\left|P\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\psi}_{n}^{\rm eff}-\psi(T^{\star}))}{v_{\rm eff}}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|\;\leq\;O\!\left(C_{A}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}}{n}}\right),

(F.17)

which vanishes under the CLT condition
C_{A}^{2}\bar{d}\log^{2c}\bar{d}/n\to 0
, i.e., (
4.11
).

Remark F.5
(Leading term vs. remainder).

For both estimators, the leading-term Berry–Esseen error is
O(\sqrt{\bar{d}/n})
, independent of
C_{A}
.
The difference between the whitened and efficient estimators lies entirely in the remainder: the whitened remainder has no
C_{A}
factor, while the efficient remainder carries the factor
C_{A}
, reflecting the additional estimation error from inverting the information operator.

G
Application to Tensor Completion

Although this paper focuses on pairwise comparisons and the new challenges they introduce (identification constraints, non-constant Fisher information, and the non-commutativity bottleneck), the semiparametric framework developed here applies to general sampling mechanisms, observation patterns, and inference targets.
To illustrate this point, we show in this section how the framework recovers and extends classical tensor completion results. Below we first outline the key steps.

Lower bounds and efficiency gaps in existing work.

The semiparametric efficiency bound
V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\psi)=\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma,(P_{\mathbb{T}}GP_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma\rangle
reveals that existing debiased estimators in the tensor completion literature—under heteroscedastic, non-additive noise, or non-uniform sampling—are not semiparametrically efficient.
The reason is that these estimators are all special cases of the score-whitening method, which replaces the information operator
G
by a scalar multiple of the identity. This achieves the whitened variance
V_{\mathrm{ws}}\geq V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, with strict inequality whenever the Fisher information
I(\eta^{\star})
varies across entries.

Whitening methods as natural adaptation.

For additive sub-exponential noise with heteroscedastic variance, the natural residual score
s(y,\eta)=y-\eta
automatically satisfies the whitening property with
I(\eta)\equiv 1
, so the score-whitened estimator is in fact fully efficient—and this is precisely the estimator used throughout the existing literature. For non-uniform sampling, IPW adjustment restores isotropy of the information operator, again reducing
G
to a scalar multiple of the identity. For 1-bit observations (e.g., logistic link), the score-whitening method of Section
5
applies identically to the pairwise case, yielding the first general CLT for debiased inference in 1-bit tensor completion.

Efficient estimation beyond whitening.

When the noise variance or sampling distribution is known (or can be estimated consistently), one can instead solve the full information equation
P_{\mathbb{T}}GP_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{H}=P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
with the true
G
operator and construct the efficient one-step estimator that achieves
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, strictly improving upon whitening whenever the information geometry is heterogeneous.
The remaining analysis is identical to the pairwise comparison case, since these observation and sampling patterns affect only the information operator
G
, and the entire asymptotic theory of Sections
4
–
5
is stated in terms of
G
abstractly.
We note that for additive noise with unknown variance, the information operator
G
involves the unknown noise variances and is harder to estimate reliably; in this case, the whitening approach is preferred.

For general observation models such as tensor completion with additive noise, we assume the following signal strength and noise conditions:

Assumption G.1
(Signal strength and sample size—general case).

The Frobenius norm and the sample size satisfy

\|T^{\star}\|_{F}\geq c\sigma\sqrt{d^{\star}}

(G.1)

for an absolute constant
c>0
, and

n\geq C_{0}\,\bar{d}\log^{c}\bar{d}

(G.2)

for a sufficiently large constant
C_{0}
depending on
\mu,\kappa,r,m
.

Assumption G.2
(Score regularity—general case).

The score function
s_{\eta}(y,\eta):=\partial_{\eta}\ell(y,\eta)
satisfies:
(i) centering:
\mathbb{E}^{\star}[s_{\eta}(Y,\eta^{\star})\mid X]=0
;
(ii) Fisher bounds:
c_{I}\leq I(\eta^{\star})\leq C_{I}
a.s.;
(iii) derivative bounds:
|\dot{s}_{\eta}(y,\eta)|\leq B_{1}
,
|\ddot{s}_{\eta}(y,\eta)|\leq B_{2}
;
(iv) tail control:
s_{\eta}(Y,\hat{\eta})
is conditionally sub-exponential with bounded scale parameter.

In the pairwise-comparison (and 1-bit) setting, both assumptions are automatically satisfied under the bounded signal condition
\|T^{\star}\|_{\infty}\leq B
(see Section
4.2
).

In this section we specialize our general framework to entrywise matrix/tensor completion. We first derive the diagonal Gram (Fisher) operator and state the semiparametric efficiency bound. We then show that existing debiased estimators in the literature are special cases of the score-whitening method and identify when they do and do not achieve full efficiency. For observation models where the information operator can be estimated (1-bit observations, additive noise with known variance), we construct the semiparametrically efficient one-step estimator that strictly improves upon whitening. Finally, we show how score whitening handles heteroscedastic noise, Bernoulli observations, and non-uniform sampling at the optimal sample complexity.

G.1
Entrywise observation model and the Gram operator

We specialize to the matrix case
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
with the Frobenius inner product and canonical basis
\{E_{jk}\}_{1\leq j\leq d_{1},\,1\leq k\leq d_{2}}
, where
[E_{jk}]_{ab}=\mathbf{1}\{(a,b)=(j,k)\}
.
In entrywise tensor completion, each sample
i
selects an entry
(j_{i},k_{i})
from a sampling distribution on
[d_{1}]\times[d_{2}]
with

p_{jk}:=\mathbb{P}\bigl((j_{i},k_{i})=(j,k)\bigr),\qquad p=d_{1}d_{2},

and we set the design
X^{(i)}=E_{j_{i}k_{i}}
.
Conditional on
X^{(i)}
, the response
Y^{(i)}
depends on
T^{\star}
only through the scalar parameter
\eta^{(i)}=\langle T^{\star},X^{(i)}\rangle=T^{\star}_{j_{i}k_{i}}
, with a scalar log-likelihood
\ell(y,\eta)
.
The per-entry score and Fisher information are

s_{\eta}(y,\eta):=\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\ell(y,\eta),\qquad I(\eta):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[s_{\eta}(Y,\eta)^{2}\,\bigm|\,\eta\bigr].

Diagonal Gram operator.

Since
X=E_{jk}
with probability
p_{jk}
, we have
X_{jk}^{2}=X_{jk}\in\{0,1\}
and the Gram operator acts diagonally:

G(H)=\sum_{j,k}\lambda_{jk}\,H_{jk}\,E_{jk},\qquad\lambda_{jk}:=p_{jk}\,I\bigl(T^{\star}_{jk}\bigr).

(G.3)

Each weight
\lambda_{jk}
is the product of two factors: the
sampling probability

p_{jk}
and the
Fisher information

I(T^{\star}_{jk})
at the true entry value. The Gram norm is
\|H\|_{G}^{2}=\sum_{j,k}\lambda_{jk}H_{jk}^{2}
.

Efficiency bound.

The full-model information equation
G(H_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{full}})=\Gamma
is solved entrywise:
(H_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{full}})_{jk}=\Gamma_{jk}/\lambda_{jk}
.
In the low-rank model, as in the pairwise case (Section
3
), we define the restricted information operator
A:=P_{\mathbb{T}}\circ G\circ P_{\mathbb{T}}
, and the semiparametric efficiency bound for
\psi(T)=\langle\Gamma,T\rangle_{F}
is

V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\psi)=\bigl\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma),\,A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\bigr\rangle_{F}.

(G.4)

G.2
When
G
is close to the identity

When the weights
\lambda_{jk}
are approximately constant—for instance, under uniform sampling (
p_{jk}=1/p
) with homoscedastic noise (
I(\eta)\equiv c
)—the Gram operator satisfies
G=(c/p)\,\mathrm{Id}
and trivially commutes with
P_{\mathbb{T}}
.
In this regime, the general one-step estimator of Section
4
achieves the semiparametric efficiency bound at the optimal sample complexity
n\gtrsim\bar{d}\,\mathrm{polylog}(\bar{d})
.

More generally, two routes to full efficiency are available (cf. Section
4
):

(a)

If the sampling probabilities
\{p_{jk}\}
and Fisher weights
\{I(T^{\star}_{jk})\}
satisfy
\max_{jk}\lambda_{jk}/\min_{jk}\lambda_{jk}\leq B_{0}(\mu,r,\kappa)
for a dimension-independent constant
B_{0}
, then
G
is a bounded perturbation of the identity. The constant
C_{A}
in (
4.16
) remains
O(1)
, and the one-step estimator achieves the bound (
G.4
) at the statistically optimal sample complexity.

(b)

Even with substantial heterogeneity, the sample complexity
n\geq C\bar{d}^{2}\,(\log\bar{d})
absorbs the non-commutativity cost
C_{A}
, so the general estimator again achieves full efficiency.

Remark G.3
(Uniform sampling with Gaussian noise).

Under uniform sampling
p_{jk}=1/p
and Gaussian noise with variance
\sigma^{2}
, we have
s_{\eta}(y,\eta)=(y-\eta)/\sigma^{2}
,
I(\eta)=1/\sigma^{2}
, and
\lambda_{jk}=1/(\sigma^{2}p)
.
The Gram operator is
G=(1/\sigma^{2}p)\,\mathrm{Id}
, the efficiency bound reduces to
V_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{MC}}=\sigma^{2}p\,\|P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\|_{F}^{2}
, and the efficient debiased estimator is

\widehat{\psi}_{\mathrm{MC}}=\langle\Gamma,\hat{T}\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}p\,\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma),X_{i}\rangle\,(Y_{i}-\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle),

recovering existing results in the matrix completion literature.

G.3
Semiparametric efficiency bound and gaps in existing literature

We now show that the semiparametric efficiency bound from our general framework reveals a precise efficiency gap in existing tensor completion inference methods under heterogeneous settings.

G.3.1
The efficiency bound

The semiparametric efficiency bound for the linear functional
\psi(T)=\langle\Gamma,T\rangle
is

V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\psi)=\bigl\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma),\,A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\bigr\rangle_{F},\qquad A=P_{\mathbb{T}}\circ G\circ P_{\mathbb{T}}.

Since
G
is diagonal with weights
\lambda_{jk}=p_{jk}I(T^{\star}_{jk})
(cf. (
G.3
)), solving the full-model information equation gives
(H_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{full}})_{jk}=\Gamma_{jk}/\lambda_{jk}
.
In the low-rank model, the efficient direction
H^{\star}=A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)
additionally incorporates the tangent-space projection, and the resulting variance
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
is strictly smaller than any variance achievable by estimators that do not solve this operator equation.

G.3.2
Why existing debiased estimators are not efficient

Existing debiased estimators for tensor completion, including those of
Ma and Xia 2024
, are all based on the residual score
s(y,\eta)=y-\eta
(for additive noise) or the whitened score
\tilde{s}=s/I(\eta)
(for non-additive noise).
In both cases, the effective Gram operator reduces to a scalar multiple of the identity:
G_{0}=c\,\mathrm{Id}
on the tangent space. The oracle direction becomes
H_{\rm ws}^{\star}=c^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
, and the resulting asymptotic variance is

V_{\rm ws}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\left[\frac{\langle H_{\rm ws}^{\star},X\rangle^{2}}{I(\eta^{\star})}\right].

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the definition of
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, one always has
V_{\rm ws}\geq V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, with equality if and only if
I(\eta^{\star})
is constant across all observed entries.

In the following cases, the inequality is strict:

•

Heteroscedastic additive noise:
When the noise variance
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
varies across entries, the whitened score
s=y-\eta
has
I(\eta)\equiv 1
, so
V_{\rm ws}=V_{\mathrm{eff}}
—the whitening approach is automatically efficient.

•

1-bit observations (e.g., logistic link):
The Fisher information
I(\eta)=f^{\prime}(\eta)^{2}/[f(\eta)(1-f(\eta))]
varies with
\eta=T^{\star}_{jk}
. Here
V_{\rm ws}>V_{\mathrm{eff}}
whenever the entries of
T^{\star}
are not all identical.

•

Non-uniform sampling with additive noise:
If one applies IPW whitening, the effective variance depends on both the sampling probabilities and the noise variances. The whitened estimator adapts to sampling heterogeneity but may not achieve the true efficiency bound.

G.3.3
Constructing the efficient estimator

When the information operator
G
can be estimated consistently, one can construct the semiparametrically efficient one-step estimator that achieves
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
.

G.3.4
Additive noise with known (or estimable) variance

Under the additive model
Y=T^{\star}_{jk}+\varepsilon
with
\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon\mid X=E_{jk})=\sigma^{2}_{jk}
, the score is
s(y,\eta)=y-\eta
and the Fisher information at entry
(j,k)
is
I_{jk}=1/\sigma^{2}_{jk}
.
With sampling probability
p_{jk}
, the Gram operator acts diagonally:

G(H)_{jk}=\frac{p_{jk}}{\sigma^{2}_{jk}}\,H_{jk}.

(G.5)

The weights
\lambda_{jk}=p_{jk}/\sigma^{2}_{jk}
combine both the sampling intensity and the signal-to-noise ratio at each entry. The Gram norm is
\|H\|_{G}^{2}=\sum_{j,k}(p_{jk}/\sigma^{2}_{jk})\,H_{jk}^{2}
.

G.3.4.1
Efficiency bound.

The restricted information operator is
A=P_{\mathbb{T}}\circ G\circ P_{\mathbb{T}}
, and the semiparametric efficiency bound is

V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\psi)=\bigl\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma),\,A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\bigr\rangle_{F}.

Under uniform sampling (
p_{jk}=1/p
) and homoscedastic noise (
\sigma^{2}_{jk}\equiv\sigma^{2}
), this simplifies to
V_{\mathrm{eff}}=\sigma^{2}p\,\|P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\|_{F}^{2}
, recovering the standard result (Remark
G.3
).

G.3.4.2
The efficient one-step estimator.

When the variances
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
are known, we construct
\hat{G}(H)_{jk}=(p_{jk}/\sigma^{2}_{jk})\,H_{jk}
and compute

\hat{\psi}_{\rm eff}=\langle\Gamma,\hat{T}\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle)\,\langle\hat{H}_{\rm eff},X_{i}\rangle,\qquad\hat{H}_{\rm eff}=(\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{G}\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma.

(G.6)

The analysis follows the proof of Theorem
4.5
exactly: the score is
s=Y-\hat{\eta}
, the score perturbation is
s(Y,\hat{\eta})-s(Y,\eta^{\star})=-\langle\Delta,X\rangle
with no Taylor remainder (since
s
is affine in
\eta
), and the H-direction bias is
-\langle\Delta,G(\hat{H}_{\rm eff}-H_{\rm eff}^{\star})\rangle
.
The resolvent perturbation argument (Appendix
C.10
) handles the error from
\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\neq P_{\mathbb{T}}
in the operator inversion.
The asymptotic variance is
V_{\mathrm{eff}}=\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma,A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma\rangle
.

Under heteroscedastic noise,
V_{\mathrm{eff}}<V_{\rm ws}
strictly. To see this, note that the whitened estimator uses
H_{\rm ws}^{\star}=p\,P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
(under uniform sampling) with variance
V_{\rm ws}=p\sum_{jk}\sigma^{2}_{jk}\,(P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma)_{jk}^{2}
, while the efficient estimator adapts its direction entry by entry via
G
, placing more weight on entries with smaller noise.

G.3.4.3
When noise variances are unknown.

When
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
is unknown, estimating
G
requires estimating each
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
, which is difficult without repeated observations at the same entry. In this case, the whitening approach (Section
G.4.1
) is preferred: the residual score
s=Y-\eta
automatically has effective Fisher information
I\equiv 1
, making the whitened estimator fully efficient
without
knowing the noise distribution. This is why the residual-based debiased estimators used throughout the existing literature are in fact optimal for additive noise.

G.3.5
1-bit observations with known link

Under the 1-bit model
Y\in\{0,1\}
with
\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\eta)=f(\eta)
for a known link
f
(e.g., logistic
f(\eta)=(1+e^{-\eta})^{-1}
) and uniform sampling
p_{jk}=1/p
, the score and Fisher information are

s(y,\eta)=\frac{(y-f(\eta))\,f^{\prime}(\eta)}{f(\eta)(1-f(\eta))},\qquad I(\eta)=\frac{f^{\prime}(\eta)^{2}}{f(\eta)(1-f(\eta))}.

The Gram operator is diagonal with entry-dependent weights:

G(H)_{jk}=\frac{1}{p}\,I(T^{\star}_{jk})\,H_{jk}.

(G.7)

Unlike the additive noise case, the Fisher information
I(T^{\star}_{jk})
varies with the latent entry value, making the Gram operator genuinely non-isotropic.

G.3.5.1
The efficient one-step estimator.

Since the link function
f
is known and
T^{\star}
can be estimated via
\hat{T}
, one can construct a consistent plug-in estimator of
G
:

\hat{G}(H)_{jk}=\frac{1}{p}\,I(\hat{T}_{jk})\,H_{jk}.

The entrywise error is controlled by the entrywise accuracy of
\hat{T}
: under the bounded-signal assumption
\|T^{\star}\|_{\infty}\leq B
and Lipschitz continuity of
I(\cdot)
,

|\hat{\lambda}_{jk}-\lambda_{jk}|=\frac{1}{p}|I(\hat{T}_{jk})-I(T^{\star}_{jk})|\leq\frac{L_{I}}{p}\,|\hat{T}_{jk}-T^{\star}_{jk}|,

where
L_{I}
is the Lipschitz constant of
I
on
[-B-1,B+1]
.
Thus the entrywise guarantee
\|\hat{T}-T^{\star}\|_{\infty}=o(1)
(already required for the one-step estimator) ensures consistency of
\hat{G}
.

The efficient one-step estimator for 1-bit completion is

\hat{\psi}_{\rm eff}=\langle\Gamma,\hat{T}\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}s(Y_{i},\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle)\,\langle\hat{H}_{\rm eff},X_{i}\rangle,\qquad\hat{H}_{\rm eff}=(\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{G}\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma.

(G.8)

The remaining analysis follows the proof of Theorem
4.5
verbatim: the additional error from
\hat{G}\neq G
is handled by the resolvent perturbation argument (Appendix
C.10
), and the CLT holds under the same sample-complexity conditions.

G.3.5.2
Asymptotic variance.

The efficient variance is

V_{\mathrm{eff}}=\bigl\langle P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma),\,A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\bigr\rangle_{F}=p\sum_{j,k}\frac{[P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)]_{jk}^{2}}{I(T^{\star}_{jk})}\cdot\frac{[A^{-1}P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)]_{jk}}{[P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)]_{jk}/I(T^{\star}_{jk})},

which is strictly smaller than the whitened variance
V_{\rm ws}=p\sum_{jk}[P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)]_{jk}^{2}/I(T^{\star}_{jk})
whenever the Fisher information
I(T^{\star}_{jk})
varies across entries.
Intuitively, the efficient estimator optimally reweights contributions from each entry according to both the tangent-space geometry and the local Fisher information, whereas the whitening approach treats all entries equally after normalization.

G.3.5.3
Comparison with whitening.

The whitened estimator for 1-bit observations (Section
G.4.2
) uses
\tilde{s}(y,\eta)=s(y,\eta)/I(\eta)
and the simplified direction
\hat{H}_{\rm ws}=p\,\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma
.
It achieves
V_{\rm ws}=p\sum_{jk}[P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)]_{jk}^{2}/I(T^{\star}_{jk})\geq V_{\mathrm{eff}}
.
The gap
V_{\rm ws}-V_{\mathrm{eff}}
grows with the heterogeneity of
I(T^{\star}_{jk})
across entries.
However, the whitening approach has the practical advantage of not requiring operator inversion (
\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{G}\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}
), making it more robust in small-sample regimes.
This is the first CLT for debiased inference in 1-bit tensor completion under general link functions.

G.4
Score whitening for tensor completion

When
G
departs substantially from the identity and the sample size is not large enough for route (b) above, the score-whitening method of Section
5
provides valid inference at the optimal sample complexity, albeit with a potentially larger variance.
We treat two important observation models.

G.4.1
Additive noise

Suppose the observation model is
Y=T^{\star}_{jk}+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon
has mean zero and possibly entry-dependent variance
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
; the noise distribution may be sub-Gaussian, sub-exponential, or otherwise—crucially, we do
not
require knowledge of
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
or even of the noise family.

The key observation is that the score for additive noise is simply

s(y,\eta)=y-\eta,

regardless of the noise distribution.
The whitened score of Section
5
is
\tilde{s}(y,\eta)=s(y,\eta)/I(\eta)
.
For Gaussian noise with variance
\sigma^{2}_{jk}
, the “true” efficient score is
(Y-\eta)/\sigma^{2}_{jk}
and the Fisher information is
I(\eta)=1/\sigma^{2}_{jk}
; dividing the two gives
\tilde{s}=Y-\eta
, i.e., the unknown variance cancels.
More generally, for
any
additive noise model one can use the score
s=Y-\eta
directly.
Since
\mathbb{E}[s^{2}\mid\eta]=\sigma^{2}_{jk}
and
\mathbb{E}[\partial_{\eta}s\mid\eta]=-1
, the whitened score satisfies properties (i)–(ii) of Section
5.1
with
I(\eta)\equiv 1
in the “natural” parameterization.

The resulting Gram operator under score whitening and uniform sampling is

G_{0}(H)=\frac{1}{p}\,H,\qquad A_{0}=\frac{1}{p}\,P_{\mathbb{T}},\qquad A_{0}^{-1}=p\,P_{\mathbb{T}}.

The score-whitened one-step estimator takes the form

\hat{\psi}_{\rm ws}=\langle\Gamma,\hat{T}\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle)\,\langle\hat{H}_{\rm ws},X_{i}\rangle,\qquad\hat{H}_{\rm ws}=p\,\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma.

(G.9)

Since
I(\eta)\equiv 1
under the natural score, the whitened estimator is
fully semiparametrically efficient
:
V_{\rm ws}=V_{\rm eff}
, regardless of heteroscedasticity (Theorem
5.2
).
Under homoscedastic noise (
\sigma^{2}_{jk}\equiv\sigma^{2}
), the variance simplifies to
V_{\rm ws}=\sigma^{2}p\,\|P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\|_{F}^{2}
.

Remark G.4
(Why the noise distribution is irrelevant).

The estimator (
G.9
) uses only the residual
Y_{i}-\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle
as the score, requiring no knowledge of the noise variance, distribution family, or heteroscedastic structure.
This is a distinctive advantage of the score-whitening approach for additive noise models: full semiparametric efficiency is achieved with a single, distribution-free estimator.

G.4.2
Bernoulli / 1-bit observations

In 1-bit matrix completion,
Y\in\{0,1\}
with
\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid\eta)=f(\eta)
for some link function
f
(e.g., logistic
f(\eta)=(1+e^{-\eta})^{-1}
). The score is
s(y,\eta)=(y-f(\eta))\,f^{\prime}(\eta)/\bigl(f(\eta)(1-f(\eta))\bigr)
and the Fisher information is

I(\eta)=\frac{f^{\prime}(\eta)^{2}}{f(\eta)(1-f(\eta))}.

The Fisher information varies with
\eta=T^{\star}_{jk}
, so the Gram operator (
G.3
) is genuinely non-isotropic.

The whitened score
\tilde{s}(y,\eta)=s(y,\eta)/I(\eta)
depends on
\eta
only, which we estimate via
\hat{T}
.
Since
I(\eta)
is a known function of the estimable parameter
\eta
, the score-whitening method of Section
5
applies with the same analysis.
The score-whitened one-step estimator is

\hat{\psi}_{\rm ws}=\langle\Gamma,\hat{T}\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{s}(Y_{i},\langle\hat{T},X_{i}\rangle)\,\langle\hat{H}_{\rm ws},X_{i}\rangle,\qquad\hat{H}_{\rm ws}=p\,\hat{P}_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma.

(G.10)

The asymptotic variance is

V_{\rm ws}=\mathbb{E}^{\star}\!\left[\frac{\langle p\,P_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma,X\rangle^{2}}{I(\eta^{\star})}\right]=p\sum_{j,k}\frac{p_{jk}\,\bigl[P_{\mathbb{T}}(\Gamma)\bigr]_{jk}^{2}}{I(T^{\star}_{jk})}.

In general
V_{\rm ws}\geq V_{\rm eff}
, with equality when
I(\eta^{\star})
is constant across all entries (i.e., when
f
is nearly linear or all entries of
T^{\star}
are close).
The CLT and remainder bounds follow from Theorem
5.2
.

G.5
Non-uniform sampling

When the sampling probabilities
\{p_{jk}\}
are non-uniform, the inverse-probability weighting (IPW) framework of Section
5.2
restores isotropy.
Specifically, choosing the uniform reference distribution
q_{jk}=1/p
and defining weights
w_{jk}=q_{jk}/p_{jk}
, the IPW-whitened estimator from (
5.14
) applies directly.
The effective Gram operator under the IPW-whitened score becomes
(1/p)\,\mathrm{Id}
, exactly as in the uniform case.

The factorization
\lambda_{jk}=p_{jk}\cdot I(T^{\star}_{jk})
in (
G.3
) makes the decomposition transparent:
score whitening
removes the Fisher information heterogeneity (Section
G.4
), while
IPW
removes the sampling heterogeneity (Section
5.2
). The two adjustments compose naturally.

When
\{p_{jk}\}
are unknown, a consistent estimator
\hat{p}_{jk}
is needed.
As discussed in Section
5.2
, if the sampling mechanism admits a low-rank or structured form, the weights can be estimated at the parametric rate with only second-order impact on the inference; see the analysis following (
5.16
).

H
Entrywise Refinement and Convex Initialization

This section contains the detailed treatment of the entrywise refinement procedure and the convex initialization discussed in Assumption
4.1
. We present three main results: (i) a row-wise refinement for matrices observed through pairwise comparisons (Appendix
H.1
), (ii) the extension to general-order tensors (Appendix
H.2
), and (iii) a nuclear-norm penalized convex program that provides the Frobenius-norm initialization in the matrix case (Appendix
H.3
).

H.1
Pairwise Matrix Refinement

Proof sketch and order-of-magnitude calculations.

The goal is to start from a Frobenius-accurate initialization
\|\widehat{T}^{(0)}-T^{\star}\|_{F}\leq C\sqrt{d^{3}/n}
and refine it to the entrywise rate
\|\widetilde{T}-T^{\star}\|_{\infty}\leq C\sqrt{d/n}\,\log^{C}d
. The proof proceeds in three stages:

Stage 1: Left-factor refinement via row-wise score equations.

We fix the right factor
\widehat{A}
(extracted from
\widehat{T}^{(0)}
by SVD) and, for each row
u\in[d]
, solve the logistic score equation
S_{u}(\theta)=0
using only comparisons involving row
u
. The key observation is that the score function at the truth decomposes as
S_{u}(\theta_{u}^{\star}+\delta)=N_{u}-H_{u}\delta-B_{u}+\text{h.o.t.}
, where
H_{u}
is a local Hessian,
N_{u}
is noise, and
B_{u}
collects bias from the imperfect right factor and offsets.
The critical order-of-magnitude calculations are: (i) each row
u
sees
m_{u}
observations with
c\,n/d\leq m_{u}\leq C\,n/d
; (ii) the design vectors
\widehat{a}_{j}
have norm
\leq Cd^{-1/2}
, so the Hessian eigenvalue satisfies
\lambda_{\min}(H_{u})\geq c\,n/d^{2}
; (iii) the noise and bias are both
\leq C(\sqrt{n}/d)\log^{C}d
. The inward-pointing zero lemma (Brouwer fixed-point argument) then guarantees a solution with error

\|\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\theta_{u}^{\star}\|\leq\frac{2R_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\leq C\,\frac{d^{2}}{n}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{d}\log^{C}d=C\,\frac{d}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{C}d.

Stage 2: Recentering and curvature amplification.

We project
\widetilde{\Theta}
onto the centered gauge:
\overline{\Theta}=P_{\perp}\widetilde{\Theta}
. This preserves pairwise differences
\overline{\theta}_{u}-\overline{\theta}_{v}=\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\widetilde{\theta}_{v}
and at most doubles the row-wise error. The crucial structural advantage is that the right-factor score equation involves the
pairwise Gram matrix

\sum_{u<v}(\overline{\theta}_{u}-\overline{\theta}_{v})(\overline{\theta}_{u}-\overline{\theta}_{v})^{\top}
, which has eigenvalue of order
n
(the full sample size) rather than
n/d^{2}
. This dramatic curvature amplification makes the right-factor refinement strictly easier.

Stage 3: Right-factor refinement and assembly.

Fixing
\overline{\Theta}
, we solve column-wise score equations for each
j\in[d]
. Thanks to the amplified curvature (
\geq c\,n
), the right-factor error satisfies
\|\widetilde{A}-A^{\star}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq C\,n^{-1/2}\log^{C}d
. Combining the two factors:

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{T}-T^{\star}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\overline{\Theta}\|_{2\to\infty}\cdot\|\widetilde{A}-A^{\star}\|_{2\to\infty}+\|\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\|_{2\to\infty}\cdot\|A^{\star}\|_{2\to\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\,\log^{C}d.

H.1.1
Problem setup, assumptions, and proof goal

Constant convention.

Throughout this subsection,
C
and
c
denote positive constants that may depend on the structural parameters
(r,\mu,\kappa,B_{0},\alpha)
but not on
d
or
n
; their values may change from line to line.

We consider a pairwise logistic matrix model with a rank-
r
signal matrix
T^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, factored as
T^{\star}=\Theta^{\star}(A^{\star})^{\top}
with
(A^{\star})^{\top}A^{\star}=I_{r}
, so the singular values are absorbed into
\Theta^{\star}
. The data consist of i.i.d. pairwise comparisons: each sample draws

J\sim\mathrm{Unif}([d]),\qquad\{U,V\}\sim\mathrm{Unif}\bigl(\{\{u,v\}:1\leq u<v\leq d\}\bigr),

and conditional on
(J,U,V)
,

Y\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}\!\left(\sigma(T^{\star}_{U,J}-T^{\star}_{V,J})\right),\qquad\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}.

Since the likelihood depends only on within-column differences, we impose the gauge condition
\mathbf{1}^{\top}T^{\star}=0
, which implies
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\Theta^{\star}=0
.

Our goal is to start from an initial estimator satisfying

\left\lVert\widehat{T}^{(0)}-T^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\Delta_{F}\leq C_{\mathrm{init}}\sqrt{d^{3}/n}

and refine it to entrywise accuracy
\left\lVert\widetilde{T}-T^{\star}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\sqrt{d/n}\,\log^{C}d
.

Assumption H.1
(Standing assumptions).

Throughout, we assume:

(A1)

r
is fixed.

(A2)

Signal strength:
c_{\sigma}\,d\leq\sigma_{r}(T^{\star})\leq\sigma_{1}(T^{\star})\leq C_{\sigma}\,d
.

(A3)

Row-wise incoherence:
\left\lVert A^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq C_{A}d^{-1/2}
and
\left\lVert\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq C_{\Theta}d^{1/2}
.

(A4)

Bounded logits:
\left\lVert T^{\star}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\alpha
, so
0<c_{\alpha}\leq\sigma^{\prime}(x)\leq C_{\alpha}
and
\left\lvert\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq L_{3}
for all relevant predictors.

(A5)

The initial estimator satisfies
\left\lVert\widehat{T}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{0}
and
\left\lVert\widehat{T}^{(0)}-T^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\Delta_{F}
(after clipping if needed).

(A6)

Sample size:
n\geq C_{n}\,d\log^{C}d
for a sufficiently large constant
C_{n}
.

H.1.2
Algorithm and proof strategy

The estimation procedure is summarized in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Pairwise matrix refinement

1.

Data splitting.
Randomly partition the data into three independent folds
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
of equal size.

2.

Initialization.
Using
D_{1}
, compute a Frobenius-accurate initializer
\widehat{T}^{(0)}
with
\|\widehat{T}^{(0)}-T^{\star}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{F}
. Extract the right factor
\widehat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
via rank-
r
SVD of
\widehat{T}^{(0)}
, followed by row-norm projection to enforce
\|\widehat{A}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq C_{A}d^{-1/2}
.

3.

Left-factor refinement.
Using
D_{2}
, for each row
u\in[d]
, solve the row-wise logistic score equation

S_{u}(\theta):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_{u}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\bigl\{z_{\ell}-\sigma(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\theta-\widehat{o}_{\ell})\bigr\}=0

to obtain
\widetilde{\theta}_{u}
. Recenter:
\overline{\Theta}:=P_{\perp}\widetilde{\Theta}
, where
P_{\perp}:=I_{d}-d^{-1}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
.

4.

Right-factor refinement.
Using
D_{3}
, for each column
j\in[d]
, solve the column-wise logistic score equation

S_{j}(a):=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}x_{t}\bigl\{Y_{t}-\sigma(x_{t}^{\top}a)\bigr\}=0,\qquad x_{t}:=\overline{\theta}_{U_{t}}-\overline{\theta}_{V_{t}},

to obtain
\widetilde{a}_{j}
.

5.

Output.
Return
\widetilde{T}:=\overline{\Theta}\,\widetilde{A}^{\top}
.

The proof has two symmetric halves—one for each factor update—connected by a recentering step. For the left update, we show that the row-wise score equation admits a solution near
\theta_{u}^{\star}
by the inward-pointing zero lemma (Lemma
H.2
): one establishes a deterministic sufficient condition involving local curvature, noise, and bias, then verifies these bounds uniformly over rows via concentration. The key estimates are:

•

Curvature:
the local Hessian satisfies
\lambda_{\min}(H_{u})\geq c\,n/d^{2}
;

•

Noise and bias:
the stochastic noise, right-factor bias, and offset bias are each

\leq C\bigl((\sqrt{n}/d)\log^{C}d\bigr)
;

•

Higher-order terms:

\beta_{u}
and
\gamma_{u}
are small enough to close the sufficient condition.

This yields
\left\lVert\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq C(d/\sqrt{n})\log^{C}d
(Proposition
H.3
).

After recentering (Lemma
H.4
), the pairwise Gram identity (Lemma
H.5
) ensures that the right-factor score equation has curvature of order
n
—far larger than the
O(n/d^{2})
curvature available for the left update. This amplification makes the right-factor refinement strictly easier, yielding

\left\lVert\widetilde{A}-A^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq C\,n^{-1/2}\log^{C}d

(Proposition
H.6
), and combining the two bounds gives the entrywise rate
\left\lVert\widetilde{T}-T^{\star}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\sqrt{d/n}\,\log^{C}d
.

H.1.3
Left-factor refinement

Lemma H.2
(Inward-pointing zero lemma).

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
be continuous. Fix
\vartheta^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\xi>0
. If

(\vartheta-\vartheta^{\star})^{\top}F(\vartheta)\leq 0\qquad\text{for all }\left\lVert\vartheta-\vartheta^{\star}\right\rVert=\xi,

then there exists
\widetilde{\vartheta}
with
F(\widetilde{\vartheta})=0
and
\left\lVert\widetilde{\vartheta}-\vartheta^{\star}\right\rVert\leq\xi
.

If
F
has no zero in
B_{\xi}(\vartheta^{\star})
, define
G(\vartheta):=\vartheta^{\star}+\xi\,F(\vartheta)/\left\lVert F(\vartheta)\right\rVert
, a continuous self-map of
B_{\xi}(\vartheta^{\star})
. By Brouwer’s theorem,
G
has a fixed point
\vartheta^{\dagger}
on the boundary, yielding
(\vartheta^{\dagger}-\vartheta^{\star})^{\top}F(\vartheta^{\dagger})=\xi\left\lVert F(\vartheta^{\dagger})\right\rVert>0
—a contradiction.
∎

We condition on
D_{1}
and work on the high-probability event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\biggl\{

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{T}^{(1)}-T^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\Delta_{F},\;\left\lVert\widehat{T}^{(1)}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{0},\;

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{A}-A^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq C\Delta_{F}/d,\;\left\lVert\widehat{A}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq C_{A}d^{-1/2},\;\left\lVert\widehat{A}^{\top}\widehat{A}-I_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq c_{0}\biggr\}.

Let
n_{2}:=|D_{2}|
.

Proposition H.3
(Left-factor refinement).

Assume
n_{2}\geq C\,d\log^{C}d
and work on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Then, with probability at least
1-d^{-c}
, the row-wise score equations admit solutions
\widetilde{\theta}_{u}
,
u\in[d]
, with

\left\lVert\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}=\max_{u\in[d]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\theta_{u}^{\star}\right\rVert\leq C\frac{d}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\log^{C}d.

Fix
u\in[d]
. Consider the observations in
D_{2}
involving row
u
, reoriented so
u
appears on the left: triples
(j_{\ell},w_{\ell},z_{\ell})
,
\ell=1,\dots,m_{u}
, with

z_{\ell}\mid(j_{\ell},w_{\ell})\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\sigma(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})),\qquad\eta_{\ell}^{\star}:=(a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star})^{\top}\theta_{u}^{\star}-o_{\ell}^{\star},\qquad o_{\ell}^{\star}:=T^{\star}_{w_{\ell}j_{\ell}},\qquad\widehat{o}_{\ell}:=\widehat{T}^{(1)}_{w_{\ell}j_{\ell}}.

Score equation and Taylor decomposition.

The row-wise score equation we solve is

S_{u}(\theta):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_{u}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\bigl\{z_{\ell}-\sigma(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\theta-\widehat{o}_{\ell})\bigr\}=0.

Since
\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\theta-\widehat{o}_{\ell}=\eta_{\ell}^{\star}+\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\delta+d_{\ell}
where
\delta:=\theta-\theta_{u}^{\star}
and
d_{\ell}:=(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}-a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star})^{\top}\theta_{u}^{\star}-(\widehat{o}_{\ell}-o_{\ell}^{\star})
, a Taylor expansion of
\sigma
around
\eta_{\ell}^{\star}
gives

S_{u}(\theta_{u}^{\star}+\delta)=N_{u}-H_{u}\delta-B_{u}^{(A)}+B_{u}^{(O)}-\mathcal{R}_{u}(\delta),

where

\displaystyle N_{u}:=\sum_{\ell}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\varepsilon_{\ell}\,(\varepsilon_{\ell}:=z_{\ell}-\sigma(\eta_{\ell}^{\star}),\;\text{centered noise}),\,H_{u}:=\sum_{\ell}\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\,\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\,(\text{local Hessian}),

\displaystyle B_{u}^{(A)}:=\sum_{\ell}\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\,\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}-a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star})^{\top}\theta_{u}^{\star}\,(\text{right-factor bias}),\,B_{u}^{(O)}:=\sum_{\ell}\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\,\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}(\widehat{o}_{\ell}-o_{\ell}^{\star})\,(\text{offset bias}),

and
\mathcal{R}_{u}(\delta):=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\,\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\ell})\,(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\delta+d_{\ell})^{2}
collects higher-order terms.

Sufficient condition from the inward-pointing lemma.

For
\left\lVert\delta\right\rVert=\xi
, the quadratic form
\delta^{\top}S_{u}(\theta_{u}^{\star}+\delta)
is bounded above by

-\lambda_{u}\xi^{2}+L_{3}\gamma_{u}\xi^{3}+R_{u}\xi,

where
\lambda_{u}:=\lambda_{\min}(H_{u})
,
\gamma_{u}:=\sup_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1}\sum_{\ell}\left\lvert\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}v\right\rvert^{3}
, and

R_{u}:=\left\lVert N_{u}\right\rVert+\left\lVert B_{u}^{(A)}\right\rVert+\left\lVert B_{u}^{(O)}\right\rVert+L_{3}\beta_{u},\qquad\beta_{u}:=\sup_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1}\sum_{\ell}\left\lvert\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}v\right\rvert\,d_{\ell}^{2}.

If
R_{u}\leq\lambda_{u}^{2}/(4L_{3}\gamma_{u})
, the expression is non-positive at
\xi_{u}=2R_{u}/\lambda_{u}
, and Lemma
H.2
gives a zero
\widetilde{\theta}_{u}
with
\left\lVert\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\theta_{u}^{\star}\right\rVert\leq 2R_{u}/\lambda_{u}
.

Curvature.

Row
u
appears in
m_{u}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n_{2},2/d)
observations. By Bernstein’s inequality applied to the scalar sum
m_{u}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}\mathbf{1}\{u\in\{U_{k},V_{k}\}\}
(independent Bernoulli summands with mean
2/d
, Bernstein radius
b=1
, and variance proxy
\sigma^{2}=n_{2}\cdot 2/d
), a union bound over
u\in[d]
gives

c\,\frac{n_{2}}{d}\leq m_{u}\leq C\,\frac{n_{2}}{d}\qquad\text{for all }u\in[d]

with probability at least
1-2d^{-10}
, provided
n_{2}\geq C\,d\log d
.
Since
\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\geq c_{\alpha}>0
and
\widehat{A}^{\top}\widehat{A}\succeq c_{0}I_{r}
on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the population Hessian satisfies

\mathbb{E}[H_{u}\mid D_{1}]\succeq c_{\alpha}\frac{n_{2}}{d}\cdot\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{a}_{j}\widehat{a}_{j}^{\top}\succeq c\frac{n_{2}}{d^{2}}I_{r}.

To pass from the population to the sample, we apply matrix Bernstein. The summands
\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\,\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}
are independent (conditional on
D_{1}
), each with operator norm
\leq C_{\alpha}\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|^{2}\leq C_{\alpha}C_{A}^{2}/d=:b_{H}
. The matrix variance proxy is

\Bigl\|\sum_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})^{2}\,\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}^{\top}\bigr]\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\alpha}^{2}\cdot\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|^{2}/d\leq C\,\frac{n_{2}}{d^{3}}.

Matrix Bernstein gives

\|H_{u}-\mathbb{E}[H_{u}\mid D_{1}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{d^{3}}\log d}+\frac{\log d}{d}\Bigr)=o\!\left(\frac{n_{2}}{d^{2}}\right)

for
n_{2}\geq C\,d\log^{C}d
, whence
\lambda_{u}\geq c\,n_{2}/d^{2}
uniformly over
u
.

Noise.

Conditionally on
D_{1}
, the summands
\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\varepsilon_{\ell}
are independent with mean zero. We apply vector Bernstein’s inequality. The independent summands are
X_{\ell}:=\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\varepsilon_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,m_{u}
. Their parameters are:

•

Bernstein radius:
b=\max_{\ell}\|X_{\ell}\|\leq\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|\cdot|\varepsilon_{\ell}|\leq C_{A}d^{-1/2}\cdot 1=C_{A}d^{-1/2}
.

•

Variance proxy: since
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{\ell}^{2}]=\sigma(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})(1-\sigma(\eta_{\ell}^{\star}))\leq 1/4
,

\sigma_{N}^{2}:=\sum_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\|X_{\ell}\|^{2}=\sum_{\ell}\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|^{2}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{\ell}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\ell}\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|^{2}\leq C\,\frac{n_{2}}{d}\cdot\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{d}=C\,\frac{n_{2}}{d^{2}}.

Vector Bernstein states
\lx@paragraphsign(\|\sum_{\ell}X_{\ell}\|\geq t)\leq(r+1)\exp(-t^{2}/(2\sigma_{N}^{2}+2bt/3))
. Setting
t=C\sqrt{(n_{2}/d^{2})\log d}+C(d^{-1/2}\log d)
and taking a union bound over
u\in[d]
:

\max_{u\in[d]}\left\lVert N_{u}\right\rVert\leq C\frac{\sqrt{n_{2}}}{d}\,\log^{C}d

with probability at least
1-d^{-10}
.

Bias.

The right-factor bias decomposes as

B_{u}^{(A)}=\mathbb{E}[B_{u}^{(A)}\mid D_{1}]+(B_{u}^{(A)}-\mathbb{E}[B_{u}^{(A)}\mid D_{1}]).

The expectation satisfies
\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[B_{u}^{(A)}\mid D_{1}]\right\rVert\leq C\,n_{2}\Delta_{A}/d^{3/2}
where
\Delta_{A}:=\left\lVert\widehat{A}-A^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq C\Delta_{F}/d
on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.

For the fluctuation, we again apply vector Bernstein. The summands are
X_{\ell}^{(A)}:=\sigma^{\prime}(\eta_{\ell}^{\star})\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}(\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}-a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star})^{\top}\theta_{u}^{\star}-\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}^{(A)}\mid D_{1}]
. These are independent, mean-zero, with:

•

Bernstein radius:
b_{A}\leq C\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|\cdot\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}-a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star}\|\cdot\|\theta_{u}^{\star}\|\leq C\,d^{-1/2}\cdot\Delta_{A}\cdot d^{1/2}=C\Delta_{A}
.

•

Variance proxy:
\sigma_{A}^{2}\leq C\,n_{2}\Delta_{A}^{2}/d^{2}
.

Vector Bernstein and a union bound over
u
give
\max_{u}\left\lVert B_{u}^{(A)}\right\rVert\leq C(\sqrt{n_{2}}/d)\log^{C}d
.

The offset bias is handled identically: the population term satisfies
\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[B_{u}^{(O)}\mid D_{1}]\right\rVert\leq C\,n_{2}\Delta_{F}/d^{5/2}
. For the fluctuation, the summands have Bernstein radius
b_{O}\leq C\|\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}\|\cdot|\widehat{o}_{\ell}-o_{\ell}^{\star}|\leq C\,d^{-1/2}\cdot C_{e}
where
|\widehat{o}_{\ell}-o_{\ell}^{\star}|\leq C_{e}
by the clipping guarantee, and variance proxy
\sigma_{O}^{2}\leq C\,n_{2}\Delta_{F}^{2}/d^{4}
. Together,
\max_{u}\left\lVert B_{u}^{(O)}\right\rVert\leq C(\sqrt{n_{2}}/d)\log^{C}d
.

Higher-order terms.

Since
\left\lvert\widehat{a}_{j}^{\top}v\right\rvert\leq Cd^{-1/2}
and
d_{\ell}^{2}\leq 2((\widehat{a}_{j_{\ell}}-a_{j_{\ell}}^{\star})^{\top}\theta_{u}^{\star})^{2}+2(\widehat{o}_{\ell}-o_{\ell}^{\star})^{2}
, we get

\max_{u}\beta_{u}\leq C\,d^{-1/2}\log^{C}d,\qquad\max_{u}\gamma_{u}\leq C\,\frac{n_{2}}{d^{5/2}}\log^{C}d.

Closing the sufficient condition.

Collecting the bounds:

R_{u}\leq C\frac{\sqrt{n_{2}}}{d}\log^{C}d,\qquad\frac{\lambda_{u}^{2}}{\gamma_{u}}\geq c\,\frac{n_{2}}{d^{3/2}}\log^{-C}d.

The sufficient condition
R_{u}\leq\lambda_{u}^{2}/(4L_{3}\gamma_{u})
holds when
n_{2}\geq C\,d\log^{C}d
. Therefore

\left\lVert\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\theta_{u}^{\star}\right\rVert\leq\frac{2R_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\leq C\,\frac{d^{2}}{n_{2}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n_{2}}}{d}\log^{C}d=C\,\frac{d}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\log^{C}d

uniformly in
u
.
∎

H.1.4
Recentering and curvature amplification

After the left update, we project onto the centered gauge to prepare for the right-factor update. The key observation is that pairwise differences amplify the curvature from
O(n/d^{2})
to
O(n)
.

Lemma H.4
(Projection to the centered gauge).

Assume
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\Theta^{\star}=0
and define
\overline{\Theta}=P_{\perp}\widetilde{\Theta}
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{\theta}_{u}-\overline{\theta}_{v}=\widetilde{\theta}_{u}-\widetilde{\theta}_{v},\,\forall u,v\in[d],

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq 2\left\lVert\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\left\lVert\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{F}.

Since
\Theta^{\star}=P_{\perp}\Theta^{\star}
,
\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}=P_{\perp}(\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star})
. Pairwise differences are preserved because the mean cancels. For the
2\to\infty
bound, let
E:=\widetilde{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}
with rows
e_{u}^{\top}
; then
(P_{\perp}E)_{u\cdot}=e_{u}-\bar{e}
where
\bar{e}=d^{-1}\sum_{v}e_{v}
, so
\left\lVert(P_{\perp}E)_{u\cdot}\right\rVert\leq 2\max_{v}\left\lVert e_{v}\right\rVert
. The Frobenius bound follows from
P_{\perp}
being an orthogonal projection.
∎

Lemma H.5
(Pairwise Gram identity).

Let
\Theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
satisfy
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\Theta=0
. Then

\sum_{1\leq u<v\leq d}(\theta_{u}-\theta_{v})(\theta_{u}-\theta_{v})^{\top}=d\,\Theta^{\top}\Theta.

The ordered sum equals
2d\,\Theta^{\top}\Theta-2(\sum_{u}\theta_{u})(\sum_{u}\theta_{u})^{\top}=2d\,\Theta^{\top}\Theta
by
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\Theta=0
. Halving gives the unordered sum.
∎

The consequence is immediate: when the right-factor score equation uses pairwise differences
x_{t}=\overline{\theta}_{U_{t}}-\overline{\theta}_{V_{t}}
as covariates, the population covariance of
x_{t}
is

\mathbb{E}\!\left[(\overline{\theta}_{U}-\overline{\theta}_{V})(\overline{\theta}_{U}-\overline{\theta}_{V})^{\top}\right]=\frac{2}{d-1}\overline{\Theta}^{\top}\overline{\Theta}\succeq c\,d\cdot I_{r},

provided
\lambda_{\min}(\overline{\Theta}^{\top}\overline{\Theta})\geq c\,d^{2}
(which holds when
\left\lVert\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}\leq c_{\Theta}\sqrt{d}
, since
c_{\sigma}^{2}d^{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\Theta^{\star\top}\Theta^{\star})=\sigma_{r}(T^{\star})^{2}\leq C_{\sigma}^{2}d^{2}
). Thus the curvature for the right update is of order
n_{3}\cdot 1\geq c\,n
—a factor of
d^{2}
larger than the
O(n/d^{2})
curvature for the left update—because each pairwise difference has norm
\leq C\sqrt{d}
rather than
\leq C\,d^{-1/2}
.

H.1.5
Right-factor refinement

Proposition H.6
(Right-factor refinement).

Under the conditions of Proposition
H.3
with
n_{3}\geq C\,d\log^{C}d
, with probability at least
1-d^{-c}
, the column-wise score equations admit solutions
\widetilde{a}_{j}
,
j\in[d]
, with

\left\lVert\widetilde{A}-A^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}=\max_{j\in[d]}\left\lVert\widetilde{a}_{j}-a_{j}^{\star}\right\rVert\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{C}d.

The argument parallels the left update but is shorter because the amplified curvature makes each term easier to control. Fix
j\in[d]
and let
\mathcal{I}_{j}:=\{t\in D_{3}:J_{t}=j\}
. By Bernstein’s inequality applied to
|\mathcal{I}_{j}|=\sum_{t\in D_{3}}\mathbf{1}\{J_{t}=j\}
(independent Bernoulli summands with mean
1/d
), we have
c\,n_{3}/d\leq|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\leq C\,n_{3}/d
uniformly over
j
with probability at least
1-2d^{-10}
. For
t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
, define

x_{t}:=\overline{\theta}_{U_{t}}-\overline{\theta}_{V_{t}},\qquad x_{t}^{\star}:=\theta_{U_{t}}^{\star}-\theta_{V_{t}}^{\star},\qquad h_{t}:=x_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}.

Score equation.

The column-wise score equation is

S_{j}(a):=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}x_{t}\{Y_{t}-\sigma(x_{t}^{\top}a)\}=0.

Writing
x_{t}^{\top}a=(x_{t}^{\star})^{\top}a_{j}^{\star}+x_{t}^{\top}\delta+h_{t}^{\top}a_{j}^{\star}
with
\delta:=a-a_{j}^{\star}
, Taylor expansion gives

S_{j}(a_{j}^{\star}+\delta)=N_{j}-H_{j}\delta-B_{j}^{(\Theta)}-\mathcal{R}_{j}(\delta),

where
N_{j}:=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}x_{t}\varepsilon_{t}
is the noise,
H_{j}:=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}\sigma^{\prime}((x_{t}^{\star})^{\top}a_{j}^{\star})\,x_{t}x_{t}^{\top}
is the Hessian,
B_{j}^{(\Theta)}:=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}\sigma^{\prime}((x_{t}^{\star})^{\top}a_{j}^{\star})\,x_{t}\,(h_{t}^{\top}a_{j}^{\star})
is the left-factor bias, and
\mathcal{R}_{j}(\delta)
collects higher-order terms. As in the left update, the inward-pointing lemma gives a zero
\widetilde{a}_{j}
with
\left\lVert\widetilde{a}_{j}-a_{j}^{\star}\right\rVert\leq 2R_{j}/\lambda_{j}
provided
R_{j}\leq\lambda_{j}^{2}/(4L_{3}\gamma_{j})
.

Curvature.

By Lemma
H.5
and the centering identity, the population Hessian satisfies

\mathbb{E}[H_{j}\mid D_{1},D_{2}]\succeq c_{\alpha}\frac{n_{3}}{d}\cdot\frac{2}{d-1}\,\overline{\Theta}^{\top}\overline{\Theta}\succeq c\,n_{3}\,I_{r}.

The factor
1/d
from column sampling is exactly compensated by the pairwise covariance being of order
d
. Each summand has operator norm
\leq C_{\alpha}\|x_{t}\|^{2}\leq C\,d
. The matrix variance proxy satisfies

\|\sum_{t}\mathbb{E}[x_{t}x_{t}^{\top}x_{t}x_{t}^{\top}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C\,n_{3}\,d
.
Matrix Bernstein gives

\left\lVert H_{j}-\mathbb{E}[H_{j}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C\bigl(\sqrt{n_{3}d\log d}+d\log d\bigr)=o(n_{3}),

whence
\lambda_{j}\geq c\,n_{3}
uniformly over
j
.

Noise.

We apply vector Bernstein to the summands
X_{t}:=x_{t}\varepsilon_{t}
. The parameters are:

•

Bernstein radius:
b=\max_{t}\|X_{t}\|\leq\|x_{t}\|\cdot|\varepsilon_{t}|\leq C\sqrt{d}\cdot 1=C\sqrt{d}
.

•

Variance proxy:
\sigma_{N}^{2}:=\sum_{t}\mathbb{E}\|X_{t}\|^{2}=\sum_{t}\|x_{t}\|^{2}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{t}^{2}]\leq C|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\cdot d\cdot\frac{1}{4}\leq C\,n_{3}
.

Vector Bernstein and a union bound over
j\in[d]
yield

\max_{j\in[d]}\left\lVert N_{j}\right\rVert\leq C\sqrt{n_{3}}\,\log^{C}d

with probability at least
1-d^{-10}
.

Bias and higher-order terms.

Set
\epsilon_{\Theta}:=\left\lVert\overline{\Theta}-\Theta^{\star}\right\rVert_{2\to\infty}
. Since
\left\lVert h_{t}\right\rVert\leq 2\epsilon_{\Theta}
and
\left\lvert h_{t}^{\top}a_{j}^{\star}\right\rvert\leq C\,\epsilon_{\Theta}/\sqrt{d}
, the bias satisfies
\max_{j}\left\lVert B_{j}^{(\Theta)}\right\rVert\leq C(n_{3}\epsilon_{\Theta}/d)\log^{C}d
. The higher-order terms satisfy
\max_{j}\beta_{j}\leq C(n_{3}\epsilon_{\Theta}^{2}/d^{3/2})\log^{C}d
and
\max_{j}\gamma_{j}\leq C\,n_{3}d^{1/2}\log^{C}d
.

Closing the sufficient condition.

Collecting the bounds:

R_{j}\leq C\left(\sqrt{n_{3}}+\frac{n_{3}\epsilon_{\Theta}}{d}+\frac{n_{3}\epsilon_{\Theta}^{2}}{d^{3/2}}\right)\log^{C}d,\qquad\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\gamma_{j}}\geq c\,\frac{n_{3}}{d^{1/2}}\log^{-C}d.
